# D.4.4 Neuron Lottery Winning:  $\mathcal{M}_i^{(0)}$

In this subsection, we prove that the neurons outside  $\mathcal{M}_i^{(0)}$  for each index  $i\in [k]$  is negligible. (They did not win the lottery so only the neurons in  $\mathcal{M}_i^{(0)}$  count.)

Lemma D.24. Suppose Parameter  $D.1$  holds and suppose Induction Hypothesis C.3 holds for all iterations  $< t$ . Then,

$$
\forall i \in [ k ], \forall \ell \in [ 2 ], \forall r \in [ m ] \setminus \mathcal {M} _ {i} ^ {(0)}: \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle \leq \widetilde {O} (\sigma_ {0})
$$

Proof of Lemma D.24. The proof is nearly identical to that of Lemma D.23.

Stage 1. In the first stage, namely when  $t \leq T_{0,i}$ , the proof is nearly identical to stage 1 of the proof of Lemma D.23. At a high level, this time we instead compare two sequences  $x_{t} = \langle w_{i,r^{*}}^{(t)}, v_{i,\ell} \rangle$  and  $y_{t} = \langle w_{i,r}^{(t)}, v_{i,\ell} \rangle$  for  $r^{*} = \arg \max_{r \in [m]} \langle w_{i,r}^{(0)}, v_{i,\ell} \rangle$  and  $r \in \mathcal{M}_{i,\ell}^{(0)}$ . We skip the details for the sake of cleanness.

Stage 2. In the second stage, namely when  $t \in [T_{0,i}, T_0]$ , we can also completely reuse the stage 2 of the proof of Lemma D.23. The only slight difference is that in order to derive (D.22), this time we instead use Induction Hypothesis C.3f.

Stage 3. In the third stage, namely when  $t \geq T_0$ , we can also completely reuse the stage 3 of the proof of Lemma D.23. The only slight difference is that in order to derive (D.22), this time we instead use Induction Hypothesis C.3f.

# D.4.5 Noise Correlation is Small

In this subsection, we prove that the neurons correlate negligibly with the random noise, that is  $\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)},\xi_p\rangle$  is small, except for those single-view data on the lottery winning views. (Recall single-view data are learned through memorization, so the learner network can correlate with their noise  $\xi_{p}$  significantly.)

Lemma D.25. Suppose Parameter D.1 holds and suppose Induction Hypothesis C.3 holds for all iterations  $< t$ . For every  $\ell \in [2]$ , for every  $r \in [m]$ , for every  $(X, y) \in \mathcal{Z}_m$  and  $i \in [k]$ , or for every  $(X, y) \in \mathcal{Z}_s$  and  $i \in [k] \setminus \{y\}$ :

(a) For every  $p \in \mathcal{P}_{v_{i,\ell}}(X)$ , we have:  $\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)}, \xi_p \rangle \leq \widetilde{o}(\sigma_0)$ .  
(b) For every  $p \in \mathcal{P}(X) \setminus \left(\mathcal{P}_{v_{i,1}}(X) \cup \mathcal{P}_{v_{i,2}}(X)\right)$ , we have:  $|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)}, \xi_p \rangle| \leq \widetilde{O}(\sigma_0)$ .  
(c) For every  $p \in [P] \setminus \mathcal{P}(X)$ , we have:  $|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)}, \xi_p \rangle| \leq \widetilde{O}(\sigma_0 \gamma k)$ .

In addition, for every  $(X,y)\in \mathcal{Z}_s$ , every  $i\in [k]$ , every  $r\in [m]$ , every  $\ell \in [2]$

(d) For every  $p \in \mathcal{P}_{v_{i,\ell}}(X)$ , if  $(i,3 - \ell) \in \mathcal{M}$  we have:  $|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)},\xi_p\rangle |\leq \widetilde{O} (\sigma_0)$ .

(e) For every  $p \in \mathcal{P}_{v_{i,\ell}}(X)$ , if  $r \in [m] \setminus \mathcal{M}_i^{(0)}$  we have:  $|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)}, \xi_p \rangle| \leq \widetilde{O}(\sigma_0)$ .

We prove Lemma D.25 after we establish the following claim.

Claim D.26. For every  $(X,y)\in \mathcal{Z}$ ,  $i\in [k]$ ,  $r\in [m]$ , and  $p\in [P]$ , suppose it satisfies  $|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)},x_p\rangle |\leq A$  for every  $t < t_0$  where  $t_0$  is any iteration  $t_0\leq T$ . Then,

- If  $(X,y)\in \mathcal{Z}_m$  then  $\left|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)},\xi_p\rangle \right|\leq \widetilde{O}\left(\frac{kA^{q - 1}}{N\sigma_0^{q - 2}} +\frac{k^5A^{q - 1}}{s^2N}\right) + \frac{\eta T}{\sqrt{d}}$  
- If  $(X, y) \in \mathcal{Z}_s$  then  $|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)}, \xi_p \rangle| \leq \widetilde{O}\left(\frac{kA^{q - 1}}{N\sigma_0^{q - 2}} + \frac{A^{q - 1}}{\rho^{q - 1}}\right) + \frac{\eta T}{\sqrt{d}}$

We first prove Lemma D.25 using Claim D.26 (which is trivial), and then we prove Claim D.26.

Proof of Lemma D.25.

- In the case of Lemma D.25a, since we have  $|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t')}, x_p \rangle| \leq \widetilde{O}(1)$  for every  $t' < t$  according to our Induction Hypothesis C.3a and C.3g, by applying Claim D.26 we immediately have  $|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)}, \xi_p \rangle| \leq \widetilde{o}(\sigma_0)$  once we plug in  $A = \widetilde{O}(1)$ ,  $N \geq \widetilde{\omega}\left(\frac{k}{\sigma_0^{q-1}}\right)$ , and  $N \geq \widetilde{\omega}\left(\frac{k^5}{\sigma_0}\right)$ .  
- In the case of Lemma D.25b, since we have  $|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t')}, x_p \rangle| \leq \widetilde{O}(\sigma_0)$  for every  $t' < t$  according to our Induction Hypothesis C.3b, by applying Claim D.26 we immediately have  $|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)}, \xi_p \rangle| \leq \widetilde{O}(\sigma_0)$  once we plug in  $A = \widetilde{O}(\sigma_0)$ ,  $N \geq k^5$  and  $(\sigma_0)^{q-2} \leq \rho^{q-1}$ .  
- In the case of Lemma D.25c, since we have  $|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t')}, x_p \rangle| \leq \widetilde{O}(\sigma_0 \gamma k)$  for every  $t' < t$  according to our Induction Hypothesis C.3c, by applying Claim D.26 we immediately have  $|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)}, \xi_p \rangle| \leq \widetilde{O}(\sigma_0 \gamma k)$  once we plug in  $A = \widetilde{O}(\sigma_0 \gamma k)$ ,  $N \geq k^5$  and  $(\sigma_0 \gamma k)^{q - 2} \leq \rho^{q - 1}$ .  
- In the case of Lemma D.25d, since we have  $|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t')}, x_p \rangle| \leq \widetilde{O}(\sigma_0)$  for every  $t' < t$  according to our Induction Hypothesis C.3e, by applying Claim D.26 we immediately have  $|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)}, \xi_p \rangle| \leq \widetilde{O}(\sigma_0)$  once we plug in  $A = \widetilde{O}(\sigma_0)$ ,  $N \geq k^5$  and  $(\sigma_0)^{q-2} \leq \rho^{q-1}$ .  
- In the case of Lemma D.25e, since we have  $|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t')}, x_p \rangle| \leq \widetilde{O}(\sigma_0)$  for every  $t' < t$  according to our Induction Hypothesis C.3f, by applying Claim D.26 we immediately have  $|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)}, \xi_p \rangle| \leq \widetilde{O}(\sigma_0)$  once we plug in  $A = \widetilde{O}(\sigma_0)$ ,  $N \geq k^5$  and  $(\sigma_0)^{q-2} \leq \rho^{q-1}$ .

Proof of Claim D.26. Recall from our earlier calculation (see (D.6)) that for every  $(X,y)\in \mathcal{Z}$  and  $p\in [P]$ , if  $y = i$  then

$$
\langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t + 1)}, \xi_ {p} \rangle = \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, \xi_ {p} \rangle + \widetilde {\Theta} \Big (\frac {\eta}{N} \Big) \widetilde {\mathrm {R e L U}} ^ {\prime} (\langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, x _ {p} \rangle) \left(1 - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} (F ^ {(t)}, X)\right) \pm \frac {\eta}{\sqrt {d}}
$$

for similar reason, if  $y \neq i$ , then

$$
\langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t + 1)}, \xi_ {p} \rangle = \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, \xi_ {p} \rangle - \widetilde {\Theta} \Big (\frac {\eta}{N} \Big) \widetilde {\mathsf {R e L U}} ^ {\prime} (\langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, x _ {p} \rangle) \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} (F ^ {(t)}, X) \pm \frac {\eta}{\sqrt {d}}
$$

Note by our assumption, we have  $\widetilde{\mathsf{ReLU}}^{\prime}(\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)},x_{p}\rangle)\leq \widetilde{O} (A^{q - 1})$ . For obvious reason, when  $t = T_0 = \Theta \left(\frac{k}{\eta\sigma_0^{q - 2}}\right)$  (see Claim D.11)

$$
\left| \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, \xi_ {p} \rangle \right| \leq \widetilde {O} \left(\frac {\eta}{N} A ^ {q - 1} T _ {0}\right) + \frac {\eta T _ {0}}{\sqrt {d}} \leq \widetilde {O} \left(\frac {k A ^ {q - 1}}{N \sigma_ {0} ^ {q - 2}}\right) + \frac {\eta T _ {0}}{\sqrt {d}}
$$

Case 1: multi-view data. We first consider  $(X,y)\in \mathcal{Z}_m$ . Using Claim D.16 and Claim D.14, we have

$$
y = i \Rightarrow \sum_ {t = T _ {0}} ^ {T} 1 - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {y} \left(F ^ {(t)}, X\right) \leq \widetilde {O} \left(\frac {k ^ {4}}{s ^ {2}}\right) \cdot \sum_ {t = T _ {0}} ^ {T} \underset {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z} _ {m}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ 1 - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {y} \left(F ^ {(t)}, X\right) \right] \leq \widetilde {O} \left(\frac {k ^ {5}}{s ^ {2} \eta}\right)
$$

$$
y \neq i \Rightarrow \quad \sum_ {t = T _ {0}} ^ {T} \operatorname {l o g i t} _ {i} (F ^ {(t)}, X) \leq \sum_ {t = T _ {0}} ^ {T} 1 - \operatorname {l o g i t} _ {y} (F ^ {(t)}, X) \leq \widetilde {O} \left(\frac {k ^ {5}}{s ^ {2} \eta}\right)
$$

Combining this with the bound at  $t = T_0$ , we have

$$
\left| \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, \xi_ {p} \rangle \right| \leq \widetilde {O} \left(\frac {k A ^ {q - 1}}{N \sigma_ {0} ^ {q - 2}} + \frac {k ^ {5} A ^ {q - 1}}{s ^ {2} N}\right) + \frac {\eta T}{\sqrt {d}}
$$

Case 2: single-view data. Let us now consider  $(X,y)\in \mathcal{Z}_s$ . Recall from Claim D.12b that

$$
\sum_ {t = T _ {0}} ^ {T} \left(1 - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {y} \left(F ^ {(t)}, X\right)\right) \leq \widetilde {\mathcal {O}} \left(\frac {N}{\eta \rho^ {q - 1}}\right)
$$

so using the same analysis, we have

$$
\left| \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, \xi_ {p} \rangle \right| \leq \widetilde {O} \left(\frac {k A ^ {q - 1}}{N \sigma_ {0} ^ {q - 2}} + \frac {A ^ {q - 1}}{\rho^ {q - 1}}\right) + \frac {\eta T}{\sqrt {d}}
$$

# D.4.6 Diagonal Correlations are Nearly Non-Negative

Lemma D.27. Suppose Parameter  $D.1$  holds and suppose Induction Hypothesis C.3 holds for all iterations  $< t$ . Then,

$$
\forall i \in [ k ] , \forall r \in [ m ] , \forall \ell \in [ 2 ]: \quad \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle \geq - \widetilde {O} (\sigma_ {0}) .
$$

Proof of Lemma D.27. Let us consider any iteration  $t$  so that  $\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)}, v_{i,\ell} \rangle \leq -\widetilde{\Omega}(\sigma_0)$ . We start from this iteration to see how negative the next iterations can be. Without loss of generality we consider the case when  $\langle w_{i,r}^{(t')}, v_{i,\ell} \rangle \leq -\widetilde{\Omega}(\sigma_0)$  holds for every  $t' \geq t$ .

By Claim D.7 and Claim D.8,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t + 1)}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle \geq \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle + \eta_ {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z}} \left[ \mathbb {1} _ {y = i} \left(V _ {i, r, \ell} (X) - \widetilde {O} (\sigma_ {p} P)\right) \left(1 - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} (F ^ {(t)}, X)\right) \right. \\ \left. - \mathbb {1} _ {y \neq i} \left(\mathcal {E} _ {1} + \mathcal {E} _ {3} + \mathbb {1} _ {v _ {i, \ell} \in \mathcal {P} (X)} V _ {i, r, \ell} (X)\right) \operatorname {l o g i t} _ {i} \left(F ^ {(t)}, X\right) \right] \\ \end{array}
$$

Recall  $V_{i,r,\ell}(X) = \sum_{p\in \mathcal{P}_{v_{i,\ell}}(X)}\widetilde{\mathrm{ReLU}}^{\prime}(\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)},x_p\rangle)z_p$ . Since  $V_{i,r,\ell}(X)\geq 0$  we can ignore it in the first occurrence corresponding to  $\mathbb{1}_{y = i}$ . Also, applying Induction Hypothesis C.3, we know that as long as  $(X,y)\in \mathcal{Z}$  and  $y\neq i$ , it satisfies

$$
V _ {i, r, \ell} (X) = \sum_ {p \in \mathcal {P} _ {v _ {i, \ell}} (X)} \widetilde {\mathrm {R e L U}} ^ {\prime} \left(\langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle z _ {p} \pm \widetilde {o} (\sigma_ {0})\right) z _ {p} = 0
$$

because we have assumed  $\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)},v_{i,\ell}\rangle \leq -\widetilde{\Omega} (\sigma_0)$ . Therefore,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t + 1)}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle \geq \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle - \eta_ {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z}} \left[ \mathbb {1} _ {y = i} \widetilde {O} (\sigma_ {p} P) (1 - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} (F ^ {(t)}, X)) \right. \\ \left. + \mathbb {1} _ {y \neq i} \left(\mathcal {E} _ {1} + \mathcal {E} _ {3}\right) \operatorname {l o g i t} _ {i} (F ^ {(t)}, X) \right] \\ \end{array}
$$

We first consider every  $t \leq T_0 \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \Theta\left(\frac{k}{\eta\sigma_0^{q-2}}\right)$  (recall Claim D.11). Using Claim D.4 we have  $\mathbf{logit}_i(F^{(t)}, X) = O\left(\frac{1}{k}\right)$ . This implies

$$
\langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle \geq - \widetilde {O} (\sigma_ {0}) - O \left(\frac {\eta T _ {0}}{k}\right) (\mathcal {E} _ {1} + \mathcal {E} _ {3}) = - \widetilde {O} (\sigma_ {0}) - O \left(\frac {1}{\sigma_ {0} ^ {q - 2}}\right) (\mathcal {E} _ {1} + \mathcal {E} _ {3}) \geq - \widetilde {O} (\sigma_ {0})
$$

(Above, the last inequality uses our earlier parameter choices, see (D.12).)

As for  $t \geq T_0$ , we combining this with  $\mathbf{logit}_i(F^{(t)},X) \leq 1 - \mathbf{logit}_y(F^{(t)},X)$  for  $i \neq y$  and  $(X,y) \in \mathcal{Z}_m$ , and  $\mathbf{logit}_i(F^{(t)},X) \leq O\left(\frac{1}{k}\right)\left(1 - \mathbf{logit}_y(F^{(t)},X)\right)$  for  $i \neq y$  and  $(X,y) \in \mathcal{Z}_s$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t + 1)}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle \geq \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle - \eta_ {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z} _ {m}} \left[ O (\mathcal {E} _ {1} + \mathcal {E} _ {3}) (1 - \operatorname {l o g i t} _ {y} (F ^ {(t)}, X)) \right] \\ - O \left(\frac {\eta N _ {s}}{N}\right) \underset {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z} _ {s}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \mathbb {1} _ {y = i} \widetilde {O} \left(\sigma_ {p} P\right) \left(1 - \operatorname {l o g i t} _ {y} \left(F ^ {(t)}, X\right)\right) \right. \\ \left. \left. + \mathbb {1} _ {y \neq i} \frac {\mathcal {E} _ {1} + \mathcal {E} _ {3}}{k} \left(1 - \operatorname {l o g i t} _ {y} (F ^ {(t)}, X)\right) \right] \right] \\ \end{array}
$$

Finally, recall

$$
\text {C l a i m} \quad D. 1 4 \Rightarrow \sum_ {t \geq T _ {0}} \mathbb {E} _ {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z} _ {m}} \left[ 1 - \operatorname {l o g i t} _ {y} (F ^ {(t)}, X) \right] \leq \widetilde {O} \left(\frac {k}{\eta}\right)
$$

$$
\text {C l a i m} \quad D. 1 2 \Rightarrow \forall i ^ {\prime} \in [ k ]: \sum_ {t \geq T _ {0}} \mathbb {E} _ {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z} _ {s}} \left[ \mathbb {1} _ {i = y} \cdot \left(1 - \operatorname {l o g i t} _ {y} (F ^ {(t)}, X)\right) \right] \leq \widetilde {O} \left(\frac {N}{\eta k \rho^ {q - 1}}\right)
$$

Therefore, we know for every  $t \in [T_0, T]$ :

$$
\langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle \geq \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(T _ {0})}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle - \widetilde {O} \big (k \cdot (\mathcal {E} _ {1} + \mathcal {E} _ {3}) \big) \stackrel {{\text {①}}} {{\geq}} \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(T _ {0})}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle - \widetilde {O} \big (\sigma_ {0} \big) \geq - \widetilde {O} \big (\sigma_ {0} \big)
$$

(Above, the inequality ① uses our earlier parameter choices, see (D.18).)

![](images/810748a430de8b3bde000156fd8e675fb8529145aa6ec3cf6caa4f3bf50b93ed.jpg)

# D.5 Putting All Together

We are now ready to restate Theorem D.2 and prove it.

Theorem D.2. Under Parameter  $D.1$ , for any  $m \in [\widetilde{\Omega}(1), \widetilde{o}(\frac{1}{\sigma_0})]$  and sufficiently small  $\eta \leq \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(k)}$ , our Induction Hypothesis C.3 holds for all iterations  $t = 0, 1, \ldots, T$ .

Proof of Theorem D.2. At iteration  $t$ , we first calculate

$$
\forall p \in P _ {v _ {j, \ell}} (X): \quad \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, x _ {p} \rangle = \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {j, \ell} \rangle z _ {p} + \sum_ {v ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {V}} \alpha_ {p, v ^ {\prime}} \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v ^ {\prime} \rangle + \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, \xi_ {p} \rangle \tag {D.25}
$$

$$
\forall p \in [ P ] \backslash P (X): \quad \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, x _ {p} \rangle = \sum_ {v ^ {\prime} \in \mathcal {V}} \alpha_ {p, v ^ {\prime}} \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v ^ {\prime} \rangle + \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, \xi_ {p} \rangle \tag {D.26}
$$

It is easy to verify Induction Hypothesis C.3 holds at iteration  $t = 0$  (merely some simple high probability bounds on Gaussian random variables). Suppose Induction Hypothesis C.3 holds for all iterations  $< t$ . We have established several lemmas

$$
\text {L e m m a} \quad \mathrm {D}. 2 2 \Rightarrow \forall i \in [ k ] \forall r \in [ m ] \forall j \in [ k ] \backslash \{i \}: \quad | \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {j, \ell} \rangle | \leq \widetilde {O} (\sigma_ {0}) \tag {D.27}
$$

$$
\text {L e m m a D . 2 1} + \text {L e m m a D . 2 7} \Rightarrow \forall i \in [ k ] \forall r \in [ m ] \forall \ell \in [ 2 ]: \quad \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle \in [ - \widetilde {O} (\sigma_ {0}), \widetilde {O} (1) ] \tag {D.28}
$$

$$
\text {L e m m a D . 2 3 + L e m m a D . 2 7} \Rightarrow \forall (i, \ell^ {*}) \in \mathcal {M} \forall r \in [ m ]: \quad | \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {i, 3 - \ell^ {*}} \rangle | \leq \tilde {\mathcal {O}} (\sigma_ {0}) \tag {D.29}
$$

$$
\text {L e m m a D . 2 7} \Rightarrow \forall i \in [ k ], \forall r \in [ m ], \forall \ell \in [ 2 ]: \quad \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle \geq - \widetilde {O} (\sigma_ {0}). \tag {D.30}
$$

$$
\text {L e m m a D . 2 4 + L e m m a D . 2 7} \Rightarrow \forall i \in [ k ], \forall \ell \in [ 2 ], \forall r \in [ m ] \backslash \mathcal {M} _ {i} ^ {(0)}: \quad | \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle | \leq \widetilde {O} (\sigma_ {0}) \tag {D.31}
$$

- To prove C.3a, it suffices to plug (D.27), (D.28) into (D.25), use  $\alpha_{p,v'} \in [0,\gamma]$ , use  $|\mathcal{V}| = 2k$ , and use  $|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)}, \xi_p \rangle| \leq \widetilde{o}(\sigma_0)$  from Lemma D.25a.  
- To prove C.3b, it suffices to plug (D.27), (D.28) into (D.25), use  $\alpha_{p,v'} \in [0,\gamma]$ , use  $|\mathcal{V}| = 2k$ , and use  $|\langle w_{j,r}^{(t)}, \xi_p \rangle| \leq \widetilde{O}(\sigma_0)$  from Lemma D.25b.  
- To prove C.3c, it suffices to plug (D.27), (D.28) into (D.26), use  $\alpha_{p,v'} \in [0,\gamma]$ , use  $|\mathcal{V}| = 2k$ , and use  $|\langle w_{j,r}^{(t)}, \xi_p \rangle| \leq \widetilde{O}(\sigma_0 \gamma k)$  from Lemma D.25c.  
- To prove C.3d, it suffices to plug (D.27), (D.28) into (D.25), use  $\alpha_{p,v'} \in [0,\gamma]$ , use  $|\mathcal{V}| = 2k$ .  
- To prove C.3e, it suffices to plug (D.27), (D.29) into (D.25), use  $\alpha_{p,v'} \in [0,\gamma]$ , use  $|\mathcal{V}| = 2k$ , and use  $|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)}, \xi_p \rangle| \leq \widetilde{O}(\sigma_0)$  from Lemma D.25d.  
- To prove C.3f, it suffices to plug (D.27), (D.31) into (D.25), use  $\alpha_{p,v'} \in [0,\gamma]$ , use  $|\mathcal{V}| = 2k$ , and use  $|\langle w_{j,r}^{(t)}, \xi_p \rangle| \leq \widetilde{O}(\sigma_0)$  from Lemma D.25e.  
- To prove C.3g, it suffices to note that (D.28) exactly implies  $\Lambda_i^{(t)} \leq \widetilde{O}(1)$ , and note that Claim D.10 (which says as long as  $\Lambda_i^{(t)} \leq O(1/m_0)$ , then it must grow by  $\Lambda_i^{(t+1)} \geq \Lambda_i^{(t)} + \Theta\left(\frac{\eta}{k}\right)\widetilde{\mathsf{ReL}}'(\Lambda_i^{(t)}))$  implies  $\Lambda_i^{(t)} \geq \Omega(\Lambda_i^{(0)}) \geq \widetilde{\Omega}(\sigma_0)$ .  
- To prove C.3h, it suffices to invoke (D.30).  
- To prove C.3i, it suffices to invoke (D.31).

![](images/2e3cf218b3fe3911e0e8ec9f96416f237a11611f422a213fa1de5c6d23b42001.jpg)

# E Single Model and Ensemble: Theorem Statements

We can now state the general version of the main theorem for single model, as below:

Theorem 1 (single model, restated). For sufficiently large  $k > 0$ , every  $m \in [\mathsf{polylog}(k), \frac{1}{s\sigma_0^q\mathsf{polylog}(k)}]$ , every  $\eta \leq \frac{1}{\mathsf{poly}(k)}$ , after  $T = \frac{\mathsf{poly}(k)}{\eta}$  many iterations, when Parameter D.1 is satisfied, with probability at least  $1 - e^{-\Omega (\log^2 k)}$ :

- (training accuracy is perfect) for every  $(X, y) \in \mathcal{Z}$ :

$$
\forall i \neq y: F _ {y} ^ {(T)} (X) \geq F _ {i} ^ {(T)} (X) + \Omega (\log k).
$$

- (multi-view testing is good) for every  $i, j \in [k]$  we have  $\widetilde{O}(1) \geq \Phi_i^{(T)} \geq 0.4\Phi_j^{(T)} + \Omega(\log k)$ , and thus

$$
\Pr_ {(X, y) \in \mathcal {D} _ {m}} \left[ F _ {y} ^ {(T)} (X) \geq \max  _ {j \neq y} F _ {j} ^ {(T)} (X) + \Omega (\log k) \right] \geq 1 - e ^ {- \Omega \left(\log^ {2} k\right)}
$$

- (single-view testing is bad) for every  $(i,\ell)\in \mathcal{M}$  we have  $\Phi_{i,3 - \ell}^{(T)}\leq \widetilde{O}\big(\sigma_0m\big)\ll \frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(k)}$  and since  $|\mathcal{M}| > k(1 - o(1))$  , we have22

$$
\Pr_ {(X, y) \in \mathcal {D} _ {s}} \left[ F _ {y} ^ {(T)} (X) \geq \max  _ {j \neq y} F _ {j} ^ {(T)} (X) - \frac {1}{\text {p o l y l o g} (k)} \right] \leq \frac {1}{2} (1 + o (1))
$$

We also state the general version of the main theorem for ensemble model, as below:

Theorem 2 (ensemble accuracy, restated). In the same setting as above, suppose  $\{F^{[w]}\}_{w\in [K]}$  are  $K$  independently randomly trained models with  $m\in [\log^{\Omega (1)}(k),\log^{O(1)}k]$  for  $T = \frac{\mathrm{poly}(k)}{\eta}$  iterations each. Let us define  $G(X) = \frac{1}{K}\sum_{w}F^{[w]}(X)$ .

- (training is perfect) same as the single model;  
- (multi-view testing is good) same as the single model;  
- (single-view testing is good) when  $K \geq \operatorname{polylog}(k)$ , ensemble model satisfies

$$
\operatorname * {P r} _ {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {D} _ {s}} \left[ G _ {y} (X) \geq \max  _ {i \in [ k ] \backslash \{y \}} G _ {i} (X) + \frac {1}{\operatorname {p o l y l o g} (k)} \right] \geq 1 - e ^ {- \Omega (\log^ {2} k)}
$$

# E.1 Proof of Theorem 1

Since Theorem D.2 implies the induction hypothesis holds for every  $t \leq T$ , we have according to Claim D.14 and Claim D.12b that

$$
\sum_ {t = T _ {0}} ^ {T} \mathbb {E} _ {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z} _ {m}} \left[ 1 - \operatorname {l o g i t} _ {y} \left(F ^ {(t)}, X\right) \right] \leq \widetilde {O} \left(\frac {k}{\eta}\right)
$$

$$
\sum_ {t = T _ {0}} ^ {T} \mathbb {E} _ {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z} _ {s}} \left(1 - \operatorname {l o g i t} _ {y} \left(F ^ {(t)}, X\right)\right) \leq \widetilde {O} \left(\frac {N}{\eta \rho^ {q - 1}}\right) \tag {E.1}
$$

Also recall that our training objective is

$$
L (F ^ {(t)}) = \mathbb {E} _ {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z}} [ - \log \operatorname {l o g i t} _ {y} (F ^ {(t)}, X) ]
$$

Now, since for every data,

- if  $\mathbf{logit}_y(F^{(t)},X)\geq \frac{1}{2}$  then  $-\log \mathbf{logit}_y(F^{(t)},X)\leq O\left(1 - \mathbf{logit}_y(F^{(t)},X)\right);$  
- if  $\mathbf{logit}_y(F^{(t)},X)\leq \frac{1}{2}$ , this cannot happen for too many tuples  $(X,y,t)$  thanks to (E.1), and when this happens we have a naive bound  $-\log \mathbf{logit}_y(F^{(t)},X)\in [0,\widetilde{O} (1)]$  using Claim D.4. Therefore, we can safely conclude using (E.1) that, when  $T\geq \mathsf{poly}(k) / \eta$

$$
\frac {1}{T} \sum_ {t = T _ {0}} ^ {T} \mathbb {E} _ {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z}} [ - \log \operatorname {l o g i t} _ {y} (F ^ {(t)}, X) ] \leq \frac {1}{\operatorname {p o l y} (k)}
$$

On the other hand, since we are using full gradient descent and the objective function is  $O(1)$ -Lipschitz continuous, it means the objective value is monotonically non-increasing. In other words, we have

$$
\mathbb {E} _ {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z}} \left(1 - \operatorname {l o g i t} _ {y} \left(F ^ {(T)}, X\right)\right) \leq \mathbb {E} _ {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z}} [ - \log \operatorname {l o g i t} _ {y} (F ^ {(T)}, X) ] \leq \frac {1}{\operatorname {p o l y} (k)}
$$

also for the last iteration  $T$ . This immediately implies that the training accuracy is perfect.

As for the multi-view test accuracy, we recall from Claim D.16 that  $0.4\Phi_i^{(T)} - \Phi_j^{(T)} \leq -\Omega (\log k)$  for every  $i \neq j$ . This combined with the function approximation Claim D.9 shows that with high probability  $F_y^{(T)}(X) \geq \max_{j \neq y} F_j^{(T)}(X) + \Omega (\log k)$  for every  $(X,y) \in \mathcal{D}_m$ .

As for the single-view test accuracy, whenever  $(i,\ell)\in \mathcal{M}$ , using Lemma D.23 we have  $\Lambda_{i,3 - \ell}^{(T)}\leq \widetilde{O} (\sigma_0)$  so  $\Phi_{i,3 - \ell}^{(T)}\leq \widetilde{O} (\sigma_0m)$ .

Now, for every single-view data  $(X,y)\in \mathcal{D}_s$  with  $y = i$ , we know that with half probability  $\widehat{\ell} (X) = 3 - \ell$ . When this happens, according to Claim D.9, we have  $F_{y}^{(T)}(X)\leq O(\rho) + \frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(k)}$  (using Def. 3.1 for the single-view distribution).

For every other  $j \neq y$ , whenever suppose  $\ell' = \arg \max_{\ell' \in [2]} \{\Phi_{j,\ell'}^{(T)}\}$ , we have  $\Phi_{j,\ell'}^{(T)} \geq \Omega(\log k)$  by a few lines above. This means, as long as  $v_{j,\ell'} \in \mathcal{V}(X)$  (which happens with probability  $s / k$  for

every  $j$ ), invoking Claim D.9 again, that  $F_{j}^{(T)}(X) \geq \widetilde{\Omega}(\Gamma)$ . In other words, when this happens for some  $j \in [k] \setminus \{i\}$  (which happens with probability at least  $1 - e^{-\Omega(\log^2 k)}$ , we have

$$
F _ {y} ^ {(T)} (X) \leq \max  _ {j \neq y} F _ {j} ^ {(T)} (X) - \frac {1}{\operatorname {p o l y l o g} (k)} \tag {E.2}
$$

To sum up, we have shown for every  $(i,\ell)\in \mathcal{M}$ , for every  $(X,y)\in \mathcal{D}_s$  with  $y = i$ , we know that with probability at least  $\frac{1}{2} (1 - o(1))$ , inequality (E.2) holds. Since the size  $|\mathcal{M}|\geq k(1 - o(1))$  (see Proposition C.2), we finish the proof.

# E.2 Proof of Theorem 2

Recall from Proposition C.2 that for every  $i \in [k]$ ,  $\ell \in [2]$  and every model  $F^{[w]}$ , the probability for  $(i, \ell)$  to be included in the set  $\mathcal{M}^{[w]}$  (defined by model  $F^{[w]}$ ) is at least  $m^{-O(1)}$ . When this happens, we also have  $\Phi_{i,\ell}^{(T)} \geq \Omega(\log k)$  for this model (because  $\Phi_i^{(T)} \geq \Omega(\log k)$  while  $\Phi_{i,3 - \ell}^{(T)} \ll 1$ ). Let us denote it as  $\Phi_{i,\ell}^{[w]}$ .

Now, for every  $(X,y)\in \mathcal{D}_s$ , with probability at least  $1 - e^{-\Omega (\log^2 k)}$ , letting  $\ell = \widehat{\ell} (X)$ , we have (see Claim D.9)

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {f o r e v e r y} F ^ {[ w ]} \mathrm {w i t h} \Phi_ {y, \ell} ^ {[ w ]} \geq \Omega (\log k) \Rightarrow F _ {y} ^ {[ w ]} (X) \geq \Phi_ {y, \ell} ^ {[ w ]} - \frac {1}{\mathsf {p o l y l o g} (k)} \geq \Omega (\log k) \\ \mathrm {f o r e v e r y} F ^ {[ w ]} \mathrm {w i t h} i \neq y \Rightarrow F _ {i} ^ {[ w ]} (X) \leq \Gamma (\Phi_ {i, 1} + \Phi_ {i, 2}) + \frac {1}{\mathsf {p o l y l o g} (k)} \leq O (\Gamma) \\ \end{array}
$$

Therefore, once we have  $K \geq m^{\Omega(1)}$  models in the ensemble, and suppose  $\Gamma \leq \frac{1}{m^{\Omega(1)}}$ , after taking average, we shall have  $G_{y}(X) \geq G_{i}(X) + \frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(k)}$  for every  $i \neq [k]$ .

# F Knowledge Distillation: Theorem Statement

In this section we show how knowledge distillation (both for ensemble and for self-distillation) can improve the final generalization accuracy. For every  $i$ , let us define the truncated scaled logit as (for  $\tau = \frac{1}{\log^2 k}$ ):

$$
\mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} ^ {\tau} (F, X) = \frac {e ^ {\min \{\tau^ {2} F _ {i} (X) , 1 \} / \tau}}{\sum_ {j \in [ k ]} e ^ {\min \{\tau^ {2} F _ {j} (X) , 1 \} / \tau}}
$$

This logit function should be reminiscent of the logit function with temperature used by the seminal knowledge distillation paper by [42]; we use the truncation function instead which is easier to analyze.

# F.1 Using Ensemble for Knowledge Distillation

Suppose  $\{F^{[i]}\}_{i\in [K]}$  are  $K = \widetilde{\Theta} (1)$  independently trained models of  $F$  for  $T = O\big(\frac{\mathrm{poly}(k)}{\eta}\big)$  iterations (i.e., the same setting as Theorem 1). Let us define their ensemble

$$
G (X) = \frac {\Xi}{K} \sum_ {i} F ^ {[ i ]} (X) \quad \text {f o r s o m e} \Xi = \widetilde {\Theta} (1) \tag {F.1}
$$

Recall from (4.3) that we train a new network  $F$  from random initialization, and at every

iteration  $t$ , we update each weight  $w_{i,r}$  by:

$$
w _ {i, r} ^ {(t + 1)} = w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)} - \eta \nabla_ {w _ {i, r}} L (F ^ {(t)}) - \eta^ {\prime} \underset {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z}} {\mathbb {E}} \left(\left(\mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} ^ {\tau} (F ^ {(t)}, X) - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} ^ {\tau} (G, X)\right) ^ {-} \nabla_ {w _ {i, r}} F _ {i} ^ {(t)} (X)\right) \qquad \qquad (4. 3) \mathrm {r e s t a t e d}
$$

Let  $F^{(t)}$  be the resulted network obtained by distilling  $G$  using algorithm (4.3) at iteration  $t$ . We have the following theorem:

Theorem 3 (ensemble distillation, restated). For sufficiently large  $k > 0$ , for every  $m \in [\log^{\Omega(1)}(k), \log^{O(1)} k]$ , every  $\eta \leq \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(k)}$ , setting  $\eta' = \eta \mathrm{poly}(k)$ , after  $T = \frac{\mathrm{poly}(k)}{\eta}$  many iterations, when Parameter G.2 is satisfied, with probability at least  $1 - e^{-\Omega(\log^2 k)}$ , for at least  $90\%$  of the iterations  $t \leq T$ :

- (training accuracy is perfect) for every  $(X, y) \in \mathcal{Z}$ :

$$
\forall i \neq y: F _ {y} ^ {(t)} (X) \geq F _ {i} ^ {(t)} (X) + \Omega (\log k).
$$

- (multi-view testing is good) for every  $i,j \in [k]$  we have  $\widetilde{O}(1) \geq \Phi_i^{(t)} \geq 0.4\Phi_j^{(t)} + \Omega(\log k)$ , and thus

$$
\operatorname * {P r} _ {(X, y) \in \mathcal {D} _ {m}} \left[ F _ {y} ^ {(t)} (X) \geq \max  _ {j \neq y} F _ {j} ^ {(t)} (X) + \Omega (\log k) \right] \geq 1 - e ^ {- \Omega (\log^ {2} k)}
$$

- (single-view testing is good) for every  $i \in [k]$  and  $\ell \in [2]$  we have  $\Phi_{i,\ell}^{(t)} \geq \Omega(\log k)$  and thus

$$
\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}\limits_{(X,y)\in \mathcal{D}_{s}}\left[F_{y}^{(T)}(X)\geq \max_{j\neq y}F_{j}^{(T)}(X) + \Omega (\log k)\right]\leq 1 - e^{-\Omega (\log^{2}k)}
$$

Our proof to Theorem 3 is in the next Section G.

# F.2 Self-Distillation: Using a Single Model to Distill Itself

Recall in the self-distillation case, we made an additional assumption for simplicity:

Assumption 4.1 (balanced  $\mathcal{D}_m$ , restated). In Def. 3.1, for multi-view data  $(X,y)$ , we additionally assume that the marginal distributions of  $\sum_{p\in \mathcal{P}_v(X)}z_p^q\in [1,O(1)]$  for  $v\in \{v_{y,1},v_{y,2}\}$ .

Let  $G$  be a single model trained in the same way as Theorem 1. At the end of training, we scale it up by a small factor  $G \gets \log^4 k \cdot G$ . Define a "lottery winning" set

$$
\mathcal {M} _ {G} \stackrel {\text {d e f}} {=} \left\{(i, \ell^ {*}) \in [ k ] \times [ 2 ] \mid \Lambda_ {i, \ell^ {*}} ^ {(0)} \geq \Lambda_ {i, 3 - \ell^ {*}} ^ {(0)} \left(1 + \frac {2}{\log^ {2} (m)}\right) \right\} \tag {F.2}
$$

which only depends on  $\Lambda_{i,\ell^*}^{(0)}$  which in terms depends on  $G$ 's random initialization. (Note  $\mathcal{M}_G$  is provably a subset of  $\mathcal{M}$  defined in (C.2).)

As for  $F$ , we break its update into two stages.

1. (Learn.) In the first stage, in the same way as Theorem 1, we start from random initialization and update

$$
w _ {i, r} ^ {(t + 1)} = w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)} - \eta \nabla_ {w _ {i, r}} L (F ^ {(t)}) \text {f o r} T = \frac {\mathsf {p o l y} (k)}{\eta} \text {i t e r a t i o n s}
$$

We let  $\mathcal{M}_F$  be the "lottery winning" set of network  $F$  at the end of stage 1 defined in the same way as (F.2) (which now depends only on  $F$ 's random initialization).

2. (Distill.) In the second stage, for another  $T' = \frac{\mathrm{poly}(k)}{\eta}$  iterations, we update

$$
w _ {i, r} ^ {(t + 1)} = w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)} - \eta_ {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z}} \left(\left(\mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} ^ {\tau} (F, X) - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} ^ {\tau} (G, X)\right) ^ {-} \nabla_ {w _ {i, r}} F _ {i} ^ {(t)} (X)\right) \quad (4. 4)
$$

Theorem 4 (self distillation, restated). Suppose the data satisfies Assumption 4.1. For sufficiently large  $k > 0$ , for every  $m \in [\log^{\Omega(1)}(k), k]$ , every  $\eta \leq \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(k)}$ , setting  $T = \frac{\mathrm{poly}(k)}{\eta}$  and  $T' = \frac{\mathrm{poly}(k)}{\eta}$ , when Parameter D.1 is satisfied, with probability at least  $1 - e^{-\Omega(\log^2 k)}$ :

- (training accuracy is perfect) for every  $(X, y) \in \mathcal{Z}$ :

$$
\forall i \neq y \colon F _ {y} ^ {(T + T ^ {\prime})} (X) \geq F _ {i} ^ {(T + T ^ {\prime})} (X) + \Omega (\log k).
$$

- (multi-view testing is good) for every  $i, j \in [k]$  we have  $\widetilde{O}(1) \geq \Phi_i^{(T + T')} \geq 0.4\Phi_j^{(T + T')} + \Omega(\log k)$ , and thus

$$
\mathop {\mathrm {P r}} _ {(X, y) \in \mathcal {D} _ {m}} \left[ F _ {y} ^ {(T + T ^ {\prime})} (X) \geq \max _ {j \neq y} F _ {j} ^ {(T + T ^ {\prime})} (X) + \Omega (\log k) \right] \geq 1 - e ^ {- \Omega (\log^ {2} k)}
$$

- (single-view testing is better) for every  $(i,\ell)\in \mathcal{M}_F\cup \mathcal{M}_G$  we have  $\Phi_{i,\ell}^{(T + T^{\prime})}\geq \Omega \bigl (\frac{1}{\log k}\bigr)$ , and since  $|\mathcal{M}_F\cup \mathcal{M}_G|\geq 1.5k(1 - o(1))$ , we have

$$
\operatorname * {P r} _ {(X, y) \in \mathcal {D} _ {s}} \left[ F _ {y} ^ {(T + T ^ {\prime})} (X) \geq \max _ {j \neq y} F _ {j} ^ {(T + T ^ {\prime})} (X) + \Omega (\log k) \right] \geq \frac {3}{4} \big (1 - o (1) \big)
$$

The proof of Theorem 4 is quite easy once the reader is familiar with the proofs of Theorem 1 and Theorem 3. We include it at the end of the next Section G.

# G Knowledge Distillation Proof for Ensemble

Our proof structure of Theorem 3 is the same as that for the single model case, but is a lot simpler thanks to our special choice of the truncated distillation function.

Specifically, we maintain the following set of simpler induction hypothesis.

Induction Hypothesis G.1. For every  $\ell \in [2]$ , for every  $r \in [m]$ , for every  $(X, y) \in \mathcal{Z}$  and  $i \in [k]$ ,

(a) For every  $p \in \mathcal{P}_{v_{i,\ell}}(X)$ , we have:  $\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)}, x_p \rangle = \langle w_{i,r}^{(t)}, v_{i,\ell} \rangle z_p \pm \widetilde{o}(\sigma_0)$ .  
(b) For every  $p \in \mathcal{P}(X) \setminus \left(\mathcal{P}_{v_{i,1}}(X) \cup \mathcal{P}_{v_{i,2}}(X)\right)$ , we have:  $|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)}, x_p \rangle| \leq \widetilde{O}(\sigma_0)$ .  
(c) For every  $p\in [P]\setminus \mathcal{P}(X)$ , we have:  $|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)},x_p\rangle |\leq \widetilde{O} (\sigma_0\gamma k)$ .

Moreover, we have for every  $i\in [k]$  , every  $\ell \in [2]$

(g)  $\Phi_{i,\ell}^{(t)}\geq \Omega (\sigma_0)$  and  $\Phi_{i,\ell}^{(t)}\leq \widetilde{O} (1)$  
(h) for every  $r \in [m]$ , it holds that  $\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)}, v_{i,\ell} \rangle \geq -\widetilde{O}(\sigma_0)$ .

(Recall  $\Phi_{i,\ell}^{(t)}\stackrel {\mathrm{def}}{=}\sum_{r\in [m]}[\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)},v_{i,\ell}\rangle ]^{+}.$ )

Parameter G.2. The parameter range for our proofs in this section to hold is the same as Parameter D.1, except that

-  $N \geq \eta T \cdot \mathsf{poly}(k)$  and  $\eta T \geq \mathsf{poly}(k)$ . (Instead of  $\eta T \geq N \cdot \mathsf{poly}(k)$ .)

Explanation: single models need a longer training time because they need to memorize single-view data; instead, here ensemble distillation can truly learn all the training data so the training time  $T$  can be shorter.[23]

-  $m = \operatorname{polylog}(k)$ .

Explanation: we do not need the model to have too much over-parameterization.

-  $\eta' = \eta \mathrm{poly}(k)$ .

Theorem G.3. Under Parameter  $G.2$ , for any  $m = \mathsf{polylog}(k)$  and sufficiently small  $\eta \leq \frac{1}{\mathsf{poly}(k)}$  and  $\eta' = \eta \mathsf{poly}(k)$ , our Induction Hypothesis  $G.1$  holds for all iterations  $t = 0, 1, \ldots, T$ .

This entire section is denoted to proving Theorem G.3, and we shall explain in the end of this section how Theorem G.3 implies Theorem 3.

Disclaimer. To make this paper more concise, in the rest of this section we highlight the key technical claims/lemmas that we need to prove Induction Hypothesis G.1. Some of the proofs we give in this section are more "sketched" because we assume the readers are already familiar with our proof languages used in Section D.

# G.1 Gradient Calculations and Function Approximation

Claim G.4. There exists some parameter  $\Xi = \mathsf{polylog}(k)$  in (F.1) so that for every  $(X,y)\in \mathcal{Z}_m$

$$
\forall i \in [ k ] \colon \quad \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} ^ {\tau} (G, X) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \frac {1}{s (X)} - k ^ {- \Omega (\log k)}, & \text {i f v _ {i , 1} o r v _ {i , 2} i s i n \mathcal {V} (X) ;} \\ k ^ {- \Omega (\log k)}, & \text {i f n e i t h e r v _ {i , 1} n o r v _ {i , 2} i s i n \mathcal {V} (X) ;} \end{array} \right.
$$

where  $s(X)$  is the number of indices  $i \in [k]$  such that  $v_{i,1}$  or  $v_{i,2}$  is in  $\mathcal{V}(X)$ . (Recall with high probability  $s(X) = \Theta(s)$ .) And, for every  $(X,y) \in \mathcal{Z}_s$ ,

$$
\forall i \in [ k ] \colon \quad \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} ^ {\tau} (G, X) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} 1 - k ^ {- \Omega (\log k)}, & i f i = y; \\ k ^ {- \Omega (\log k)}, & i f i \neq y; \end{array} \right.
$$

Proof of Claim G.4. Using the same analysis as the proof of Theorem 2, we know after ensemble, for every  $i \in [k]$  and  $\ell \in [2]$ ,

$$
\frac {1}{K} \sum_ {w \in [ K ]} \Phi_ {i, \ell} ^ {[ w ]} \geq \frac {1}{\operatorname {p o l y l o g} (k)}
$$

and therefore there exists some scale-up factor  $\Xi = \mathsf{polylog}(k)$  for (F.1) so that for every  $(X,y)\in \mathcal{Z}_m$ , every  $i\in [k]$ ,

-  $G_{i}(X) \geq \log^{4} k$  when either  $v_{i,1}$  or  $v_{i,2}$  is in  $\mathcal{V}(X)$ ;  
-  $G_{i}(X) \leq 1$  when neither  $v_{i,1}$  nor  $v_{i,2}$  is in  $\mathcal{V}(X)$ .

and at the same time, for every  $(X,y)\in \mathcal{Z}_s$ , every  $i\in [k]$

-  $G_{i}(X) \geq \log^{4} k$  when  $i = y$ ;  
-  $G_{i}(X) \leq 1$  when  $i \neq y$ .

Plugging this into the threshold logit function (4.2) finishes the proof.

Note that our update rule (4.3) is not precisely the gradient of a function (due to our truncation to the negative part for simpler analysis). However, in the remainder of the proof, slightly abusing notation, let us denote by

$$
\nabla_ {w _ {i, r}} \widetilde {L} (F; X, y) \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \nabla_ {w _ {i, r}} L (F; X, y) - \frac {\eta^ {\prime}}{\eta} \left(\mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} ^ {\tau} (F, X) - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} ^ {\tau} (G, X)\right) ^ {-} \nabla_ {w _ {i, r}} F _ {i} (X)
$$

so that when

Fact G.5. Given data point  $(X,y)\in \mathcal{D}$ , for every  $i\in [k]$ ,  $r\in [m]$ , up to a negligible additive error  $\frac{1}{k^{\Omega(\log k)}}$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \begin{array}{c} - \nabla_ {w _ {i, r}} \widetilde {L} (F; X, y) = \left(\mathbb {1} _ {y = i} - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} (F, X) + \frac {\eta^ {\prime}}{\eta} \mathbb {1} _ {v _ {i, 1}, v _ {i, 2} \in \mathcal {V} (X)} \left(\frac {1}{s (X)} - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} ^ {\tau} (F, X)\right) ^ {+}\right) \nabla_ {w _ {i, r}} F _ {i} (X) \\ \hskip 1 4. 2 2 6 3 7 8 p t \text {w h e n} (X, y) \in \mathcal {Z} _ {m} \end{array} \\ \begin{array}{c} - \nabla_ {w _ {i, r}} \widetilde {L} (F; X, y) = \left(\mathbb {1} _ {y = i} - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} (F, X) + \frac {\eta^ {\prime}}{\eta} \mathbb {1} _ {y = i} \left(1 - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} ^ {\tau} (F, X)\right) ^ {+}\right) \nabla_ {w _ {i, r}} F _ {i} (X) \\ \hskip 2 8. 4 5 2 7 5 6 p t \text {w h e n} (X, y) \in \mathcal {Z} _ {s} \end{array} \\ \end{array}
$$

where recall  $\nabla_{w_{i,r}}F_i(X) = \sum_{p\in [P]}\widetilde{\mathsf{ReLU}}'(\langle w_{i,r},x_p\rangle)x_p$

Because  $\frac{1}{k^{\Omega}(\log k)}$  is negligible, for proof simplicity, we ignore it in the rest of the proof.

We also summarize a simple calculation that is analogous to Claim D.7 and Claim D.8.

Claim G.6 (gradient, c.f. Claim D.7 and D.8). For every  $t \leq T$ , for every  $(X,y) \in \mathcal{Z}$ , every  $i \in [k]$ ,  $r \in [m]$  and  $\ell \in [2]$ , we have:

- If  $v_{i,1}, v_{i,2} \in \mathcal{V}(X)$  then  $\left\langle \nabla_{w_{i,r}} F_i^{(t)}(X), v_{i,\ell} \right\rangle \geq \left(V_{i,r,\ell}(X) - \widetilde{O}(\sigma_p P)\right)$ .  
$\left\langle \nabla_{w_{i,r}}F_i^{(t)}(X),v_{i,\ell}\right\rangle \leq \left(\mathbb{1}_{v_{i,\ell}\in \mathcal{V}(X)}V_{i,r,\ell}(X) + \mathcal{E}_1 + \mathcal{E}_3\right)$  
for every  $j\in [k]\setminus \{i\}$  ,  $\left|\left\langle -\nabla_{w_{i,r}}F_i^{(t)}(X),v_{j,\ell}\right\rangle \right|\leq (\mathcal{E}_{2,i,r}(X) + \mathcal{E}_1 + \mathcal{E}_3 + \mathcal{E}_{4,j,\ell}(X))$

Notation. Throughout the remainder of the proof, let us use  $v_{i,1}, v_{i,2} \in \mathcal{V}(X)$  to denote that at least one of  $v_{i,1}, v_{i,2}$  is in  $\mathcal{V}$ . This simplifies our notations.

Recall

$$
\Phi_ {i, \ell} ^ {(t)} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \sum_ {r \in [ m ]} [ \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle ] ^ {+} \quad \mathrm {a n d} \quad \Phi_ {i} ^ {(t)} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \sum_ {\ell \in [ 2 ]} \Phi_ {i, \ell} ^ {(t)}
$$

and this time we have

Claim G.7 (function approximation, c.f. Claim D.9). Under the new Induction Hypothesis G.1, let us define  $Z_{i,\ell}^{(t)}(X) \coloneqq \mathbb{1}_{v_{i,\ell} \in \mathcal{V}(X)}\left(\sum_{p \in \mathcal{P}_{v_{i,\ell}}(X)} z_p\right)$ , we have: for every  $t$ , every  $i \in [k]$ , every  $(X,y) \in \mathcal{Z}$  (or for every new sample  $(X,y) \sim \mathcal{D}$ , with probability at least  $1 - e^{-\Omega (\log^2 k)}$ ):

$$
F _ {i} ^ {(t)} (X) = \sum_ {\ell \in [ 2 ]} \left(\Phi_ {i, \ell} ^ {(t)} \times Z _ {i, \ell} ^ {(t)} (X)\right) \pm \widetilde {O} (\sigma_ {0} \cdot m) = \sum_ {\ell \in [ 2 ]} \left(\Phi_ {i, \ell} ^ {(t)} \times Z _ {i, \ell} ^ {(t)} (X)\right) \pm O (\frac {1}{\mathrm {p o l y l o g} (k)})
$$

# G.2 Useful Claims as Consequences of the Induction Hypothesis

Recall we had five useful claims in Section D.2 for the proof of the single model case. This time, we only have three and they are also easier than their counterparts in Section D.2.

# G.2.1 Lambda Growth

Claim G.8 (growth, c.f. Claim D.10). Suppose Induction Hypothesis G.1 holds at iteration  $t$ , then for every  $i \in [k]$ ,  $\ell \in [2]$ , suppose  $\Phi_{i,\ell}^{(t)} \leq \frac{1}{\tau}$ , then it satisfies

$$
\Phi_ {i, \ell} ^ {(t + 1)} \geq \Phi_ {i, \ell} ^ {(t)} + \widetilde {\Omega} \left(\frac {\eta^ {\prime}}{k}\right) \widetilde {\operatorname {R e L U}} ^ {\prime} (\Phi_ {i, \ell} ^ {(t)})
$$

Proof of Claim D.10. Using the same calculation as (D.5), but this time substituting the new gradient formula in Fact G.5, we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t + 1)}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle \geq \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle - O (\eta + \eta^ {\prime} \frac {N _ {s}}{N}) \\ + \Omega (\eta^ {\prime}) \underset {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z} _ {m}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \mathbb {1} _ {v _ {i, 1}, v _ {i, 2} \in \mathcal {V} (X)} \left(V _ {i, r, \ell} (X) - \widetilde {O} (\sigma_ {p} P)\right) \left(\frac {1}{s (X)} - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} ^ {\tau} (F ^ {(t)}, X)\right) ^ {+} \right] \\ \end{array}
$$

Let us now consider  $r\in [m]$  to be the arg  $\max_{r\in [m]}\left\{\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)},v_{i,\ell}\rangle \right\}$ , so we have  $\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)},v_{i,\ell}\rangle \geq \widetilde{\Omega} (\Phi_{i,\ell}^{(t)})$

Following a similar analysis as before, we can derive that as long as  $\mathbb{1}_{v_{i,1}, v_{i,2} \in \mathcal{V}(X)} = 1$ , we have  $V_{i,r,\ell}(X) \geq \Omega(1) \cdot \widetilde{\mathrm{ReL}}^{\prime}\left(\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)}, v_{i,\ell} \rangle\right) \geq \widetilde{\Omega}\big(\widetilde{\mathrm{ReL}}^{\prime}(\Phi_{i,\ell}^{(t)})\big)$ .

Now, since  $\Phi_{i,\ell}^{(t)}\leq \frac{1}{\tau}$ , we know that as long as  $v_{i,\ell}\in \mathcal{V}(X)$  and  $v_{i,3 - \ell}\notin \mathcal{V}(X)$  (which happens for  $\Theta (\frac{s}{k})$  fraction of the multi-view training data), it satisfies (see Claim G.7):

$$
F _ {i} ^ {(t)} (X) \leq \Phi_ {i, \ell} ^ {(t)} \times Z _ {i, \ell} ^ {(t)} (X) - \widetilde {O} (\sigma_ {0} m) = O \Big (\frac {1}{\tau} \Big) - \widetilde {O} (\sigma_ {0} m) \leq O \Big (\frac {1}{\tau} \Big) .
$$

When this happens, we know  $\mathbf{logit}_i^\tau (F^{(t)},X)\leq O(\frac{1}{k})$ . This implies, after summing over  $r\in [m]$

$$
\Phi_ {i, \ell} ^ {(t + 1)} \geq \Phi_ {i, \ell} ^ {(t)} + \widetilde {\Omega} \left(\frac {\eta^ {\prime}}{k}\right) \widetilde {\operatorname {R e L U}} ^ {\prime} (\Phi_ {i, \ell} ^ {(t)})
$$

![](images/3c8845c81fce69c915f83864f322db0f7e34fb3277c2993912f50d976721b6d7.jpg)

Now we can define  $T_0$  as follows.

Claim G.9. Define iteration threshold  $T_0 \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \widetilde{\Theta}\left(\frac{k}{\eta'\sigma_0^{q-2}}\right)$ , then

for every  $i\in [k],\ell \in [2]$  and  $t\geq T_0$  , it satisfies  $\Phi_{i,\ell}^{(t)}\geq \frac{1}{2\tau}$

# G.2.2 Single-View Error Till the End

Claim G.10 (single-view after  $T_0$ ). Suppose Induction Hypothesis G.1 holds for all iterations  $< t$  and  $t \geq T_0$ . For every single-view data  $(X, y) \in \mathcal{Z}_s$  (or any  $(X, y) \in \mathcal{D}_s$  but with probability  $1 - e^{-\Omega (\log^2 k)}$ ), we have

$$
F _ {y} ^ {(t)} (X) \geq \max  _ {i \in [ k ] \backslash \{y \}} F _ {i} ^ {(t)} (X) + \Omega (\log k) \quad a n d \quad 1 - \operatorname {l o g i t} _ {y} (F ^ {(t)}, X) \leq \frac {1}{\operatorname {p o l y} (k)}
$$

Proof. This is because for single-view data  $(X,y)\in \mathcal{Z}_s$ , it satisfies  $Z_{i,\ell}^{(t)}(X)\leq \Gamma$  as long as  $i\neq y$ . As a result, applying Claim G.9, we must have  $F_{y}^{(t)}(X)\geq \Omega (\frac{1}{\tau})\geq \Omega (\log k)$  but  $F_{i}^{(t)}(X)\leq O(1)$  for  $i\neq y$  (using  $\Gamma < \frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(k)}$ ). (Similar for  $(X,y)\in \mathcal{D}_s$ .)

# G.2.3 Multi-View Error Till the End

Claim G.11 (multi till the end, c.f. Claim D.14). Suppose Induction Hypothesis G.1 holds for every iteration  $t < T$ , then

-  $\eta \sum_{t = T_0}^T\mathbb{E}_{(X,y)\sim \mathcal{Z}_m}\left[\mathbb{1}_{y = i}\bigl {(}1 - \mathbf{logit}_y(F^{(t)},X)\bigr)\right]\leq \widetilde{O} (1)$  
$\frac{\eta'N_s}{N}\sum_{t = T_0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{(X,y)\sim \mathcal{Z}_s}\left[\mathbb{1}_{y = i}\left(1 - \mathbf{logit}_i^\tau (F^{(t)},X)\right)^+\right]\leq \widetilde{O} (1)$  
$\eta^{\prime}\sum_{t = T_0}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{(X,y)\sim \mathcal{Z}_m}\left[\mathbb{1}_{v_{i,1},v_{i,2}\in \mathcal{V}(X)}\left(\frac{1}{s(X)} -\mathbf{logit}_i^\tau (F^{(t)},X)\right)^+\right]\leq \widetilde{O} (1)$

Proof of Claim G.11. By Fact G.5 and Claim G.6 again (similar to the calculation in the proof of Claim G.8), we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t + 1)}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle \geq \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle - \frac {1}{\operatorname {p o l y} (k)} \\ + \Omega (\eta) \underset {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z} _ {m}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \mathbb {1} _ {y = i} \left(V _ {i, r, \ell} (X) - \widetilde {O} (\sigma_ {p} P)\right) \left(1 - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} (F ^ {(t)}, X)\right) \right] \\ + \Omega \left(\frac {\eta^ {\prime} N _ {s}}{N}\right) _ {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z} _ {s}} \mathbb {E} _ {\left[ \mathbb {1} _ {y = i} \left(V _ {i, r, \ell} (X) - \widetilde {O} (\sigma_ {p} P)\right) \left(1 - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} ^ {\tau} (F ^ {(t)}, X)\right) ^ {+} \right]} \\ + \Omega (\eta^ {\prime}) \underset {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z} _ {m}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \mathbb {1} _ {v _ {i, 1}, v _ {i, 2} \in \mathcal {V} (X)} \left(V _ {i, r, \ell} (X) - \widetilde {O} (\sigma_ {p} P)\right) \left(\frac {1}{s (X)} - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} ^ {\tau} (F ^ {(t)}, X)\right) ^ {+} \right] \\ \end{array}
$$

In the above formula, we can ignore the single-view data for the  $(1 - \mathbf{logit}_i(F^{(t)},X))$  term because they are extremely small (see Claim G.10).

Now, if we take  $r = \arg \max_{r\in [m]}\{\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)},v_{i,\ell}\rangle \}$ , we must have (whenever  $v_{i,1},v_{i,2}\in \mathcal{V}(X)$ )  $V_{i,r,\ell}(X)\geq \Omega (1)\cdot \widetilde{\mathsf{ReLU}}^{\prime}\Big(\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)},v_{i,\ell}\rangle \Big)\geq \widetilde{\Omega} (1)$ . Therefore, when summing up over all possible  $r\in [m]$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \Phi_ {i, \ell} ^ {(t + 1)} \geq \Phi_ {i, \ell} ^ {(t)} - \frac {\eta}{\operatorname {p o l y} (k)} \\ + \widetilde {\Omega} (\eta) \underset {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z} _ {m}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \mathbb {1} _ {y = i} \left(1 - \operatorname {l o g i t} _ {i} (F ^ {(t)}, X)\right) \right] \\ + \widetilde {\Omega} \left(\frac {\eta^ {\prime} N _ {s}}{N}\right) _ {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z} _ {s}} \mathbb {E} _ {\left[ \mathbb {1} _ {y = i} \left(1 - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} ^ {\tau} (F ^ {(t)}, X)\right) ^ {+} \right]} \\ + \widetilde {\Omega} (\eta^ {\prime}) \underset {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z} _ {m}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \mathbb {1} _ {v _ {i, 1}, v _ {i, 2} \in \mathcal {V} (X)} \left(\frac {1}{s (X)} - \operatorname {l o g i t} _ {i} ^ {\tau} (F ^ {(t)}, X)\right) ^ {+} \right] \\ \end{array}
$$

After telescoping, and using  $\Phi^{(t)}\leq \widetilde{O} (1)$  , we finish the proof.

![](images/eeba224e5071c86a9c92ae374bca56b7a099d3ec5e612e0c5d751f0c9f2fe84b.jpg)

# G.3 Main Lemmas for Proving the Induction Hypothesis

In this subsection, we provide key technical lemmas that, when combined together, shall prove that Induction Hypothesis G.1 holds for every iteration (and thus prove Theorem G.3).<sup>24</sup>

# G.3.1 Correlation Growth

Lemma G.12 (c.f. Lemma D.21). Suppose Parameter G.2 holds and suppose Induction Hypothesis G.1 holds for all iterations  $< t$ . Then, letting  $\Phi_{i,\ell}^{(t)} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \sum_{r \in [m]} [\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)}, v_{i,\ell} \rangle]^{+}$ , we have

$$
\forall i \in [ k ], \forall \ell \in [ 2 ]: \quad \Phi_ {i, \ell} ^ {(t)} \leq \widetilde {O} (1)
$$

Proof of Lemma G.12. Let us denote by  $\Phi^{(t)} = \max_{i\in [k],\ell \in [2]}\Phi_{i,\ell}^{(t)}$ . Suppose  $t$  is some iteration so that  $\Phi^{(t)}\geq \frac{10}{\tau^2}$  but  $\Phi^{(t)}\leq \widetilde{O} (1)$ . We wish to prove that if we continue from iteration  $t$  for at most  $T$  iterations, then  $\Phi^{(t')}\leq \widetilde{O} (1)$  for every  $t'\in [t,T]$ .

Without loss of generality, we assume that  $\Phi^{(t)}\geq \frac{10}{\tau^2}$  always holds from iteration  $t$  onwards (because otherwise we can start with the next iteration  $t^\prime$  so that  $\Phi^{(t)}$  goes above  $\frac{10}{\tau^2}$ .)

Let  $(i,\ell) = \arg \max_{i\in [k],\ell \in [2]}\Phi_{i,\ell}^{(t)}$ . Then, for every  $(X,y)\in \mathcal{Z}_m$ , (G.7) tells us that  $F_{i}^{(t)}(X)\geq \frac{1}{\tau^2}$  for every  $i$  such that  $v_{i,1},v_{i,2}\in \mathcal{V}(X)$ . Therefore,

$$
\mathbb {1} _ {v _ {i, 1}, v _ {i, 2} \in \mathcal {V} (X)} \left(\frac {1}{s (X)} - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} ^ {\tau} (F, X)\right) ^ {+} \leq \frac {1}{k ^ {\Omega (\log k)}}
$$

Also, for every  $(X,y)\in \mathcal{Z}_s$ , for similar reason we have

$$
(1 - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} ^ {\tau} (F, X)) ^ {+} \leq \frac {1}{k ^ {\Omega (\log k)}}
$$

Therefore, at this iteration  $t$ , up to negligible  $\frac{1}{k^{\Omega(\log k)}}$  terms, we have according to Claim G.7:

$$
- \nabla_ {w _ {i, r}} \widetilde {L} (F; X, y) = \left(1 - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} (F, X)\right) \nabla_ {w _ {i, r}} F _ {i} (X) \qquad \text {w h e n} i = y
$$

$$
- \nabla_ {w _ {i, r}} \widetilde {L} (F; X, y) = - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} (F, X) \nabla_ {w _ {i, r}} F _ {i} (X) \qquad \text {w h e n} i \neq y
$$

This is already identical to what we had in the single-model case (without distillation).

This time, we can calculate (using Claim G.6)

$$
\begin{array}{l} \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t + 1)}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle = \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle + \eta_ {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z}} \left[ \langle - \nabla_ {w _ {i, r}} \widetilde {L} (F ^ {(t)}; X, y), v _ {i, \ell} \rangle \right] \\ \leq \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle + \eta_ {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z}} \left[ \mathbb {1} _ {y = i} (V _ {i, r, \ell} (X) + \mathcal {E} _ {1} + \mathcal {E} _ {3}) (1 - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} (F, X)) \right. \\ \leq \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle + O (\eta) \underset {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \mathbb {1} _ {y = i} \left(1 - \operatorname {l o g i t} _ {y} (F, X)\right) \right] \\ \end{array}
$$

- For every  $(X, y) \in \mathcal{Z}_s$ , since  $\Phi_{i,\ell}^{(t)} \geq \frac{10}{\tau^2}$ , it satisfies (similar to Claim G.10) that  $F_y^{(t)}(X) \geq \Omega\left(\frac{1}{\tau^2}\right) \gg \Omega(\log^2 k)$  but  $F_j^{(t)}(X) \leq O(1)$  for  $j \neq y$  (using  $\Gamma < \frac{1}{\mathrm{polylog}(k)}$ ). This implies

$$
\left(1 - \operatorname {l o g i t} _ {y} (F, X)\right) \leq \frac {1}{k ^ {\Omega (\log k)}}
$$

- For every  $(X, y) \in \mathcal{Z}_m$  with  $y = i$ , recall from Claim G.7 that

$$
F _ {j} ^ {(t)} (X) = \sum_ {\ell \in [ 2 ]} \left(\Phi_ {j, \ell} ^ {(t)} \times \mathbb {1} _ {v _ {j, \ell} \in \mathcal {V} (X)} \left(\sum_ {p \in \mathcal {P} _ {v _ {j, \ell}} (X)} z _ {p}\right)\right) \pm O (\frac {1}{\operatorname {p o l y l o g} (k)})
$$

By our choice of the distribution, this implies

-  $F_{j}^{(t)}(X)\leq 0.8001\Phi^{(t)}$  for  $j\neq i$  , and  
-  $F_{i}^{(t)}(X)\geq 0.9999\Phi^{(t)}$  because  $(i,\ell)$  is the argmax of  $\Phi_{i,\ell}^{(t)}$

This again means

$$
\left(1 - \operatorname {l o g i t} _ {y} (F, X)\right) \leq \frac {1}{k ^ {\Omega (\log k)}}
$$

Together, and summing up over all  $r \in [m]$ , we have

$$
\Phi^ {(t + 1)} \leq \Phi^ {(t)} + \frac {\eta m}{k ^ {\Omega (\log k)}}
$$

so if we continue this for  $T$  iterations we still have  $\Phi^{(T)}\leq \widetilde{O} (1)$

# G.3.2 Off-Diagonal Correlations are Small

Lemma G.13 (c.f. Lemma D.22). Suppose Parameter G.2 holds and suppose Induction Hypothesis G.1 holds for all iterations  $< t$ . Then,

$$
\forall i \in [ k ], \forall r \in [ m ], \forall j \in [ k ] \backslash \{i \}: \quad | \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {j, \ell} \rangle | \leq \widetilde {O} (\sigma_ {0})
$$

Proof of Lemma G.13. We separately treat  $t \leq T_0$  and  $t \geq T_0$ . (This should be reminiscent of the three-stage proof in the single model case.)

Consider  $t \leq T_0$ . By Fact G.5 and Claim G.6

$$
\begin{array}{l} | \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t + 1)}, v _ {j, \ell} \rangle | \leq | \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {j, \ell} \rangle | + O (\eta + \frac {\eta^ {\prime} N _ {s}}{N}) \\ + O \left(\eta^ {\prime}\right) _ {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z} _ {m}} \left[ \left(\mathcal {E} _ {2, i, r} (X) + \mathcal {E} _ {1} + \mathcal {E} _ {3} + \mathcal {E} _ {4, j, \ell} (X)\right) \mathbb {1} _ {v _ {i, 1}, v _ {i, 2} \in \mathcal {V} (X)} \left(\frac {1}{s (X)} - \operatorname {l o g i t} _ {i} ^ {\tau} (F, X)\right) ^ {+} \right] \\ \end{array}
$$

Using the property that  $v_{i,1}, v_{i,2} \in \mathcal{V}(X)$  with probability  $\Theta\left(\frac{s}{k}\right)$  over a sample  $(X,y) \in \mathcal{Z}_m$ , and using the trivial bound  $\left(\frac{1}{s(X)} - \mathbf{logit}_i^\tau(F,X)\right)^+ \leq \Theta\left(\frac{1}{s}\right)$ , we immediately have

$$
\begin{array}{l} | \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t + 1)}, v _ {j, \ell} \rangle | \leq | \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {j, \ell} \rangle | + O (\eta + \frac {\eta^ {\prime} N _ {s}}{N}) \\ + O \left(\frac {\eta^ {\prime}}{k}\right) (\gamma + (\sigma_ {0} ^ {q - 1}) \gamma s + \widetilde {O} ((\sigma_ {0} \gamma k) ^ {q - 1}) \gamma P + (\sigma_ {0}) ^ {q - 1} \frac {s}{k}) \\ \end{array}
$$

Finally, using  $T_0 = \widetilde{\Theta}\left(\frac{k}{\eta'\sigma_0^{q - 2}}\right)$ ,  $N_{s} \leq \frac{N}{\mathrm{poly}(k)}$ , and using  $\eta \leq \frac{\eta'}{\mathrm{poly}(k)}$ , together with the same parameter choices as before, we conclude that  $|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t + 1)},v_{j,\ell}\rangle |\leq \widetilde{O} (\sigma_0)$  for every  $t \leq T_0$ .

Consider  $t > T_0$ . By Fact G.5 and Claim G.6 again

$$
\begin{array}{l} | \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t + 1)}, v _ {j, \ell} \rangle | \leq | \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {j, \ell} \rangle | + O (\frac {\eta}{\operatorname {p o l y} (k)}) \\ + O (\eta) \underset {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z} _ {m}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left(\mathcal {E} _ {2, i, r} (X) + \mathcal {E} _ {1} + \mathcal {E} _ {3} + \mathcal {E} _ {4, j, \ell} (X)\right) \mathbb {1} _ {y = i} \left(1 - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} (F, X)\right) \right] \\ + O (\eta) \underset {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z} _ {m}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left(\mathcal {E} _ {2, i, r} (X) + \mathcal {E} _ {1} + \mathcal {E} _ {3} + \mathcal {E} _ {4, j, \ell} (X)\right) \mathbb {1} _ {y \neq i} \left(\operatorname {l o g i t} _ {i} (F, X)\right) \right] \\ + O \left(\frac {\eta^ {\prime} N _ {s}}{N}\right) \underset {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z} _ {s}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left(\mathcal {E} _ {2, i, r} (X) + \mathcal {E} _ {1} + \mathcal {E} _ {3} + \mathcal {E} _ {4, j, \ell} (X)\right) \mathbb {1} _ {y = i} \left(1 - \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} ^ {\tau} (F, X)\right) ^ {+} \right] \\ + O \left(\eta^ {\prime}\right) \underset {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z} _ {m}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left(\mathcal {E} _ {2, i, r} (X) + \mathcal {E} _ {1} + \mathcal {E} _ {3} + \mathcal {E} _ {4, j, \ell} (X)\right) \mathbb {1} _ {v _ {i, 1}, v _ {i, 2} \in \mathcal {V} (X)} \left(\frac {1}{s (X)} - \operatorname {l o g i t} _ {i} ^ {\tau} (F, X)\right) ^ {+} \right] \\ \end{array}
$$

In the above formula, we can ignore the single-view data for the  $(1 - \mathbf{logit}_y(F^{(t)},X))$  and  $\mathbf{logit}_i(F^{(t)},X)$  (for  $i\neq y$ ) terms because they are extremely small (see Claim G.10).

Now, applying the naive upper bounds  $\mathcal{E}_{2,i,r}(X) \leq \gamma$  and  $\mathcal{E}_{4,j,\ell}(X) \leq \widetilde{O}(\sigma_0^{q-1})$ , and telescoping for all  $t \geq T_0$  and applying Claim G.11, we immediately have  $|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)}, v_{j,\ell} \rangle| \leq |\langle w_{i,r}^{(T_0)}, v_{j,\ell} \rangle| + \widetilde{O}(\sigma_0)$ .

# G.3.3 Noise Correlation is Small

Lemma G.14 (c.f. Lemma D.25). Suppose Parameter  $G$ .2 holds and suppose Induction Hypothesis  $G$ .1 holds for all iterations  $< t$ . For every  $\ell \in [2]$ , for every  $r \in [m]$ , for every  $(X, y) \in \mathcal{Z}$  and  $i \in [k]$ :

(a) For every  $p \in \mathcal{P}_{v_{i,\ell}}(X)$ , we have:  $\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)}, \xi_p \rangle \leq \widetilde{o}(\sigma_0)$ .  
(b) For every  $p \in \mathcal{P}(X) \setminus \left(\mathcal{P}_{v_{i,1}}(X) \cup \mathcal{P}_{v_{i,2}}(X)\right)$ , we have:  $|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)}, \xi_p \rangle| \leq \widetilde{O}(\sigma_0)$ .  
(c) For every  $p \in [P] \setminus \mathcal{P}(X)$ , we have:  $|\langle w_{i,r}^{(t)}, \xi_p \rangle| \leq \widetilde{O}(\sigma_0 \gamma k)$ .

Proof. From a similar calculation (see (D.6)) we have for every  $(X,y)\in \mathcal{Z}$  and  $p\in [P]$

$$
\langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t + 1)}, \xi_ {p} \rangle = \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, \xi_ {p} \rangle \pm \widetilde {\Theta} \Big (\frac {\eta^ {\prime}}{N} \Big) \widetilde {\mathsf {R e L U}} ^ {\prime} (\langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, x _ {p} \rangle) \pm \frac {\eta}{\sqrt {d}}
$$

After telescoping and using  $N \gg T$  from Parameter G.2, we immediately finish the proof. (If one instead wishes to consider the case of  $T \geq N$ , she has to do a more careful calculation here. We skip it to keep this paper concise.

# G.3.4 Diagonal Correlations are Nearly Non-Negative

Lemma G.15 (c.f. Lemma D.27). Suppose Parameter G.2 holds and suppose Induction Hypothesis G.1 holds for all iterations  $< t$ . Then,

$$
\forall i \in [ k ] , \forall r \in [ m ] , \forall \ell \in [ 2 ]: \quad \langle w _ {i, r} ^ {(t)}, v _ {i, \ell} \rangle \geq - \widetilde {O} (\sigma_ {0}) .
$$

The proof of Lemma G.15 is almost identical to Lemma D.27 so we skip here.

# G.4 Proof of Theorem 3

First of all, applying Claim G.11 and  $T \geq \frac{\mathrm{poly}(k)}{\eta}$ , we know there are at most 90% of the iterations  $t \leq T_0$  satisfying

$$
\underset {(X, y) \sim \mathcal {Z} _ {m}} {\mathbb {E}} \left[ \left(1 - \operatorname {l o g i t} _ {y} (F ^ {(t)}, X)\right) \right] \leq \frac {1}{\operatorname {p o l y} (k)}
$$

Applying Claim D.16, we immediately have the test accuracy result for multi-view data.

Applying Claim G.10 (which uses  $\Phi_{i,\ell}^{(t)}\geq \Omega (\log k)$ ), we immediately have the test accuracy result for single-view data.

# G.5 Proof of Theorem 4

We assume the readers are now familiar with the proofs of the single model Theorem 1 and of the ensemble distill Theorem 3. They easily imply Theorem 4 for reasons we explain below.

Stage 1 of  $F$ . Recall Theorem 1 (and Lemma D.21) imply that, at the end of the stage 1 for training a network  $F$ , the quantity  $\Phi_i^{(T)} \in [\Omega(\log k), \widetilde{O}(1)]$  for every  $i \in [k]$ ; thus, if  $(i, \ell) \in \mathcal{M}$ , we must have  $\Phi_{i,\ell}^{(T)} \geq \Omega(\log k)$ . At the end of stage 1, also recall for every  $(i, \ell) \in \mathcal{M}$ , for any single-view data  $(X, y) \in \mathcal{D}_s$  with  $y = i$  and  $\widehat{\ell}(X) = \ell$ , with high probability  $F$  predicts correctly on  $(X, y)$ . Let us remind the readers from (C.2) that

$$
\mathcal {M} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \left\{(i, \ell^ {*}) \in [ k ] \times [ 2 ] \Big | \Lambda_ {i, \ell^ {*}} ^ {(0)} \geq \Lambda_ {i, 3 - \ell^ {*}} ^ {(0)} \left(\frac {S _ {i , 3 - \ell^ {*}}}{S _ {i , \ell^ {*}}}\right) ^ {\frac {1}{q - 2}} \left(1 + \frac {1}{\log^ {2} (m)}\right) \right\}
$$

where  $S_{i,\ell} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \mathbb{E}_{(X,y) \sim \mathcal{Z}_m}\left[\mathbb{1}_{y=i} \sum_{p \in P_{v_{i,\ell}}(X)} z_p^q\right]$ . Since in this self-distillation theorem, we have Assumption 4.1 which says the distribution of  $\sum_{p \in \mathcal{P}_v(X)} z_p^q$  for  $v \in \{v_{y,1}, v_{y,2}\}$  are the same over multi-view data, by standard concentration, we know with high probability  $S_{i,1} = S_{i,2}\big(1 \pm \frac{1}{2\log^2 k}\big)$  for every  $i \in [k]$ . This means, we can alternatively define

$$
\mathcal {M} _ {F} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \left\{(i, \ell^ {*}) \in [ k ] \times [ 2 ] \Big | \Lambda_ {i, \ell^ {*}} ^ {(0)} \geq \Lambda_ {i, 3 - \ell^ {*}} ^ {(0)} \Big (1 + \frac {2}{\log^ {2} (m)} \Big) \right\}
$$

(which is a subset of  $\mathcal{M}$ ) and all the statements about  $\mathcal{M}$  also apply to  $\mathcal{M}_F$ .

Base Model  $G$ . For a similar reason, if  $(i,\ell) \in \mathcal{M}_G$  for the distill model  $G$ , then we have that the quantity  $\Phi_{i,\ell}^{(T)} \geq \mathrm{polylog}(k)$  for network  $G$  (recall  $G$  is scaled up by a polylog( $k$ ) factor). Using a similar analysis to Claim G.4, we can derive that:

for every  $(X,y)\in \mathcal{Z}_m$  , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \forall (i, \ell) \in \mathcal {M} _ {G}: \quad \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} ^ {\tau} (G, X) \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \geq \frac {1}{s (X)} - k ^ {- \Omega (\log k)}, & \text {i f} v _ {i, \ell} \text {i s i n} \mathcal {V} (X); \\ = k ^ {- \Omega (\log k)}, & \text {i f} v _ {i, \ell} \text {i s n o t i n} \mathcal {V} (X); \end{array} \right. (G.1) \\ \forall i \in [ k ]: \quad \mathbf {l o g i t} _ {i} ^ {\tau} (G, X) \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \leq \frac {1}{s ^ {\prime} (X)} + k ^ {- \Omega (\log k)}, & \text {i f} v _ {i, 1} \text {o r} v _ {i, 2} \text {i s i n} \mathcal {V} (X); \\ = k ^ {- \Omega (\log k)}, & \text {i f n e i t h e r} v _ {i, 1} \text {o r} v _ {i, 2} \text {i s n o t i n} \mathcal {V} (X); \end{array} \right. (G.2) \\ \end{array}
$$

where recall  $s(X)$  is the number of indices  $i \in [k]$  such that  $v_{i,1}$  or  $v_{i,2}$  is in  $\mathcal{V}(X)$ , and we newly define  $s'(X)$  as the number of indices  $i \in [k]$  such that  $(i,\ell) \in \mathcal{M}_G$  and  $v_{i,\ell} \in \mathcal{V}(X)$  for some  $\ell \in [2]$ . One can derive using concentration that with high probability  $\frac{s}{2} \leq s'(X) \leq s(X) \leq 3s$  for all multi-view training data.[25]

Stage 2 of  $F$ . Similar to the proof of Theorem 3, we can ignore single-view data's contribution to the gradient updates (since they are negligible) and only focus on multi-view data. Using a similar gradient calculation to Fact G.5, we know that, up to some small error,

- the quantity  $\Phi_{i,\ell} = \sum_{r\in [m]}\left[\langle w_{i,r},v_{i,\ell}\rangle \right]^+$  never decreases during stage 2.  
- the quantity  $\Phi_{i} = \sum_{r\in [m],\ell \in [2]}\left[\langle w_{i,r},v_{i,\ell}\rangle \right]^{+}$  no longer changes during stage 2, when it reaches  $\Phi_i\geq \frac{2}{\tau^2}$ .<sup>26</sup>

Recall after stage 1, we have  $\Phi_i \geq \Omega(\log k)$  for network  $F$ ; but since at the beginning of stage 2 we have scaled up  $F$  by a factor of  $\log^4 k$ , this means  $\Phi_i \geq \frac{2}{\tau^2}$  is already satisfied at the beginning of stage 2 (up to small error), so it does not change during stage 2. As a result, at the end of stage 2, network  $F$  should give the same (nearly perfect) accuracy on multi-view data as claimed in Theorem 1.

Furthermore, through a similar analysis to Claim G.8 and Claim G.9 (and combining with (G.1)), we know that for when  $(i,\ell)\in \mathcal{M}_G$ , the quantity  $\Phi_{i,\ell}$  must increase to at least  $\frac{1}{2\tau}\geq \Omega (\log^2 k)$ . This allows us to conclude that, when  $(i,\ell)\in \mathcal{M}_G$ , at the end of stage 2, for those single-view data  $(X,y)\in \mathcal{D}_s$  with  $y = i$  and  $\widehat{\ell} (X) = \ell$ , with high probability  $F_{y}(X)\geq \Omega (\log^{2}k)\gg F_{j}(X)$  for  $j\neq y$ , so  $F$  predicts correctly on  $(X,y)$ .

At the same time, for every  $(i,\ell)\in \mathcal{M}_F$ , we have  $\Phi_{i,\ell}\geq \Omega (\log k)$  is already satisfied at the end of stage 1, so at the end of stage 2 it must also satisfy  $\Phi_{i,\ell}\geq \Omega (\log^5 k)$  (the extra factors are due to scale-up). Thus,  $F$  also predicts correctly on those single-view data  $(X,y)\in \mathcal{D}_s$  with  $y = i$  and  $\widehat{\ell} (X) = \ell$ .

Finally, using  $|\mathcal{M}_F| \geq k(1 - o(1))$  and  $|\mathcal{M}_G| \geq k(1 - o(1))$ , together with the fact that they are totally independent random sets, we obtain  $|\mathcal{M}_F \cup \mathcal{M}_G| \geq \frac{3}{2} k(1 - o(1))$ . This means, learned model  $F^{(T + T')}$  at the end of stage 2 through self-distillation has an accuracy of  $\geq \frac{3}{4}(1 - o(1))$  over single-view data.

# H Simple Probability Lemmas

We first state a simple proposition that directly implies Fact C.1.

Proposition H.1. Given  $m$  i.i.d. standard Gaussian random variables  $g_{1},\ldots ,g_{m}\sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)$ , with probability at least  $1 - \delta$ , we have that except for at most  $O(\log (1 / \delta))$  indices  $i\in [m]$ , we have

$$
g _ {i} \leq \max _ {j \in [ m ]} \{g _ {j} \} \cdot \left(1 - \Omega \Big (\frac {1}{\log (m / \log (1 / \delta))} \Big)\right)
$$

Proof of Proposition H.1. Recall for every  $x > 0$

$$
\left(\frac {1}{x} - \frac {1}{x ^ {3}}\right) \frac {e ^ {- x ^ {2} / 2}}{\sqrt {2 \pi}} \leq \mathbf {P r} [ g > x ] \leq \frac {1}{x} \frac {e ^ {- x ^ {2} / 2}}{\sqrt {2 \pi}} \tag {H.1}
$$

The probability for one of them to exceed

$$
\mathbf {P r} [ \max _ {i} g _ {i} > x ] = 1 - (1 - \mathbf {P r} _ {g} [ g > x ]) ^ {m}
$$

Let us choose  $x^{*}$  so that  $\mathbf{Pr}[\max_{i}g_{i} > x] = 1 - \delta /2$ . By the asymptotic bound above, it is easy to derive that  $x^{*} = \Theta (\sqrt{\log(m / \log(1 / \delta))})$  and  $\mathbf{Pr}[g > x^{*}] = \Theta (\frac{\log(1 / \delta)}{m})$ .

Now, consider  $x = x^{*} - \frac{1}{x^{*}} = x^{*}(1 - \frac{1}{(x^{*})^{2}})$ . By the asymptotic bound above, it is not hard to see

$$
\mathbf {P r} [ g > x ] \leq O (1) \cdot \mathbf {P r} [ g > x ^ {*} ] \leq O (\frac {\log (1 / \delta)}{m})
$$

By Chernoff bound, we know with probability at least  $1 - \delta$ , it satisfies that

$$
\Pr \left[ \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {m} \mathbb {1} _ {g > x} \geq \Omega (\log (1 / \delta)) \right] \leq \delta / 2
$$

![](images/96eab769a39a1ba87ff750c35a442d8ecd673008ea74f36cb72b6ac455b342da.jpg)

We next state a proposition that shall be used to prove Proposition C.2.

Proposition H.2. Consider two sequences of i.i.d. Gaussian,  $g_{1},\ldots ,g_{m}\sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)$  and  $h_1,\dots ,h_m\sim$ $\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^2)$ . Then,

- when  $\sigma > 1$ , we have  $\mathbf{Pr}\left[\max_{i \in [m]} g_i > \max_{i \in [m]} h_i\right] \geq \Omega\left(\frac{1}{\sigma}\right) \frac{1}{m^{\sigma^2 - 1}}$ .  
- when  $\sigma \leq 1$ , given  $\tau > 0$ , we have  $\mathbf{Pr}\left[\max_{i \in [m]} g_i = (1 \pm O(\tau)) \max_{i \in [m]} h_i\right] \leq O(\tau \log m + \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(m)})$ .

Proof of Proposition H.2. The case of  $\sigma \leq 1$  is trivial and is simply by symmetry.

The case of  $\sigma > 1$ . For any threshold  $x > 0$ , we have

$$
\mathbf {P r} \left[ \max  _ {i} g _ {i} > x \right] = 1 - \left(1 - \underset {g \sim \mathcal {N} (0, 1)} {\mathbf {P r}} [ g > x ]\right) ^ {m}
$$

$$
\mathbf {P r} [ \max _ {i} h _ {i} <   x ] = (1 - \mathbf {P r} _ {h \sim \mathcal {N} (0, \sigma^ {2})} [ h > x ]) ^ {m}
$$

Let  $x^{*} > \sigma$  be a threshold satisfying  $\frac{\sigma}{x^{*}}\frac{e^{-(x^{*})^{2}/2\sigma^{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}} = \frac{1}{m}$ . By the earlier Gaussian tail bound (H.1), it is easy to verify

$$
\mathbf {P r} [ \max _ {i} h _ {i} <   x ^ {*} ] = (1 - \mathbf {P r} _ {h \sim \mathcal {N} (0, \sigma^ {2})} [ h > x ^ {*} ]) ^ {m} \geq \Omega (1)
$$

Using the earlier Gaussian tail bound (H.1), one can also verify that

$$
\operatorname * {P r} _ {g \sim \mathcal {N} (0, 1)} [ g > x ^ {*} ] \geq \frac {1}{2 x ^ {*}} \frac {e ^ {- (x ^ {*}) ^ {2} / 2}}{\sqrt {2 \pi}} \geq \frac {1}{2 \sigma} \left(\frac {1}{m}\right) ^ {\sigma^ {2}}
$$

Therefore,

$$
\mathbf {P r} [ \max _ {i} g _ {i} > x ^ {*} ] = 1 - (1 - \underset {g \sim \mathcal {N} (0, 1)} {\mathbf {P r}} [ g > x ^ {*} ]) ^ {m} \geq 1 - \left(1 - \Omega (m) \cdot \frac {1}{\sigma m ^ {\sigma^ {2}}}\right)
$$

Combining both, we have

$$
\mathbf {P r} [ \max _ {i} h _ {i} <   x ^ {*} <   \max _ {i} g _ {i} ] = \mathbf {P r} [ \max _ {i} h _ {i} <   x ^ {*} ] \cdot \mathbf {P r} [ \max _ {i} g _ {i} > x ^ {*} ] \geq \frac {\Omega (1)}{\sigma m ^ {\sigma^ {2} - 1}}
$$

The case of  $\sigma \leq 1$ . Let us first generate  $h$  and then generate  $g$ . Since  $\sigma \leq 1$ , we have with probability at least  $1 - \frac{1}{\mathrm{poly}(m)}$ , it satisfies that  $0 < \max_{i \in [m]} h_i \leq O(\sqrt{\log m})$ . When this happens, denoting by  $z = \max_{i \in [m]} h_i$ , we can apply a known anti-concentration result for the maximum of Gaussian variables [21, Theorem 3]:

$$
\mathbf {P r} \left[ \max _ {i \in [ m ]} g _ {i} \in [ z (1 - \tau), z (1 + \tau) ] \right] \leq O (z \tau) \cdot O (\mathbb {E} [ \max _ {i \in [ m ]} g _ {i} ]) \leq O (z \tau \sqrt {\log m})
$$

This finishes the proof.

![](images/21fc1d826a9ce70abe3dd94f157c928bf360ec8dc4e6e0456159b3b53c6fccfd.jpg)

Let us restate Proposition C.2 for the readers' convenience. Suppose we denote by  $S_{i,\ell} \stackrel{\mathrm{def}}{=} \mathbb{E}_{(X,y) \sim \mathcal{Z}_m}\left[\mathbb{1}_{y = i} \sum_{p \in P_{v_{i,\ell}}(X)} z_p^q\right]$ . Then, define

$$
\mathcal {M} \stackrel {\mathrm {d e f}} {=} \left\{(i, \ell^ {*}) \in [ k ] \times [ 2 ] \Big | \Lambda_ {i, \ell^ {*}} ^ {(0)} \geq \Lambda_ {i, 3 - \ell^ {*}} ^ {(0)} \left(\frac {S _ {i , 3 - \ell^ {*}}}{S _ {i , \ell^ {*}}}\right) ^ {\frac {1}{q - 2}} \left(1 + \frac {1}{\log^ {2} (m)}\right) \right\}
$$

Proposition C.2. Suppose  $m \leq \mathrm{poly}(k)$ . We have the following properties about  $\mathcal{M}$ .

- For every  $i \in [k]$ , at most one of  $(i, 1)$  or  $(i, 2)$  is in  $\mathcal{M}$  (obvious).

- For every  $i \in [k]$ , suppose  $S_{i,\ell} \geq S_{i,3 - \ell}$ , then

$$
\begin{array}{l} - \operatorname * {P r} \left[ (i, 3 - \ell) \in \mathcal {M} \right] \geq m ^ {- O (1)}. \\ - \Pr \left[ (i, \ell) \in \mathcal {M} o r (i, 3 - \ell) \in \mathcal {M} \right] \geq 1 - o (1) \\ \end{array}
$$

Proof of Proposition C.2. We only prove the second item since the first one is trivial. Suppose  $S_{i,\ell} \geq S_{i,3 - \ell}$ .

By our assumption on the data distribution, it is easy to verify  $S_{i,1} / S_{i,2} > 0$  is a constant for every  $i \in [k]$ . Therefore,  $(i,3 - \ell) \in \mathcal{M}$  with probability at least  $\frac{1}{m^{O(1)}}$  following the first item of Proposition H.2.

Finally, if neither  $(i,\ell)$  or  $(i,3 - \ell)$  is in  $\mathcal{M}$ , it necessarily implies

$$
\Lambda_ {i, \ell} ^ {(0)} = \Lambda_ {i, 3 - \ell} ^ {(0)} \left(\frac {S _ {i , 3 - \ell}}{S _ {i , \ell}}\right) ^ {\frac {1}{q - 2}} \left(1 \pm O \left(\frac {1}{\log^ {2} m}\right)\right)
$$

but according to the second item of Proposition H.2, this happens with probability at most  $\frac{1}{\log m}$ .

[3] Zeyuan Allen-Zhu and Yuanzhi Li. Can SGD Learn Recurrent Neural Networks with Provable Generalization? In NeurIPS, 2019. Full version available at http://arxiv.org/abs/1902.01028.
